Running it on **all days of two animals** reproduces\nthe paper's own saved Figure-1F decoding errors **exactly**:\n\n| Animal | Sessions | Reproduced mean error | Saved `within_decoding` | Max per-day difference |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| QLAK-CA1-51 | 21 | 17.567 cm | 17.567 cm | **0.0000 cm** |\n| QLAK-CA1-75 | 31 | 10.893 cm | 10.893 cm | **0.0000 cm** |\n\nThis is an end-to-end proof that my data loading, stream alignment and curation are\nidentical to the reference implementation.\n\nStep-by-step comparison of my script against the reference:\n\n| Stage | Reference | `convert_data.py` | Same? |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| (a) loading | `load_dat(animal, p, format=\"joblib\")` → `joblib.load(data/<animal>)[animal]` | identical call | ✅ |\n| (b) neuron filtering | unregistered cells are NaN and are removed implicitly; `cell_idx = sum(traces[vel_idx]) > cell_threshold(=5)` | explicit `~isnan(trace[:,0])` mask, then `raw[:, frame_keep].sum(1) > 5` | ✅ |\n| (b) neuron filtering | the paper's place-cell test (`get_place_cells`) is **not** applied before decoding | not applied | ✅ |\n| (c) temporal alignment | `trace` and `position` share one 30 Hz frame index (hardware-timestamped by the same DAQ); no further alignment | same; trials are cut on the same frame index for all three streams | ✅ |\n| (c) sample selection | `vel_idx = gaussian_filter1d(‖Δbehav‖·fps, sigma=5) > v_thresh/bin_down`, index 0 left False | same formula in cm/s (`speed > 5`), bin 0 of the session excluded | ✅ (applied after binning, see below) |\n| (d) binning (neural) | `AvgPool1d(k=s=3)` over `gaussian_filter1d(traces, sigma=3, axis=0)` | `mean` over 15-frame blocks of `gaussian_filter1d(trace, sigma=15, axis=-1)` | ⚠️ bin size 15 instead of 3 frames — justified in Step 5 decision 4 |\n| (d) binning (position) | same pooling, then `.astype(int)` after dividing by `bin_down` | same: mean position per bin, then `floor(pos/bin_down)` clipped to [0,2] | ✅ (clip added, see Check 5) |\n| (d) spatial grid | `bin_down = (max over time, axes and days + buffer)/n_bins` | identical, with `n_bins = 3` instead of 15 (task requirement) | ✅ |\n| (e) input construction | reference never builds a decoder input (its Bayes decoder uses neural data only); geometry appears via `get_env_mat` | 9-dim binary vector from `blocked`, verified identical to `flipud(get_env_mat(env))==0` | ✅ |\n| (f) output construction | class index = `x_bin*n_bins + y_bin` (`fit_decoder`'s `empty_map` flattening) | class = `y_bin*3 + x_bin` | ⚠️ transposed labelling, chosen so the output index matches the dataset's own partition numbering and hence the geometry input. A relabelling of 9 classes; it cannot change any accuracy. |\n\nTwo deliberate deviations, both documented above and in Step 5: the temporal bin size\n(500 ms instead of 100 ms, required to give the provided per-timepoint linear decoder a\nusable SNR from a 0.24 Hz event train) and the class-index transpose (required so that\ndecoder input and output share an index). One refinement: the speed filter is applied\n*after* binning rather than before, so that no time bin ever averages across a temporal\ngap — the reference pools already-filtered frames, which silently mixes non-contiguous\nsamples. Neither affects agreement with the reference results above.\n\n### Check 4: Key statistics comparison\nSee the table in Step 9 — every statistic available in the paper (subjects, sessions,\nneurons, cells per animal, rate maps, geometries, sequences, session length, sampling\nrate, arena size) is reproduced exactly. Full accounting of the data:\n\n| Quantity | Value | Explanation |\n|---|---|---|\n| frames in the raw recordings | 14,903,019 (207 sessions × 71,866–72,219) | 100 % |\n| frames in complete 1-min trials | 14,736,600 (**98.88 %**) | trailing partial minute dropped: 1,666 frames/session for CA1-30 and CA1-50 (39 trials), ≤ 219 for the other five animals (40 trials) |\n| 500 ms bins | 982,440 | 8,187 possible trial-minutes |\n| bins dropped: speed ≤ 5 cm/s | 453,142 (46.1 %) | reference criterion |\n| bins dropped: inside a blocked partition | 28 (0.003 %) | DeepLabCut artifacts; 428 of the 443 offending raw frames come from a single session (CA1-51 day 4) |\n| bins dropped with their trial (< 10 bins left) | 468 bins in 168 trials of 8,187 (2.05 % of trials, 0.05 % of bins) | minutes the mouse spent almost entirely immobile (mean 2.8 running bins each) |\n| **bins retained** | **528,802 (53.8 %)** | 73.4 h of running, in 8,019 trials |\n| registered cell-sessions | 69,744 | matches the paper |\n| cell-sessions dropped: ≤ 5 events while running | 833 (1.19 %) | reference criterion |\n| **neurons retained** | **68,911** | mean 332.9/session |\n| sessions dropped | 0 | every one of the 207 sessions survives |\n| subjects dropped | 0 | |\n\nOutput class distribution [.095 .108 .114 .088 .070 .088 .108 .158 .171]: mildly\nnon-uniform, as expected for a freely foraging mouse in a partitioned box (the centre\npartition 4 is the least occupied because it is blocked in 5 of the 10 geometries and is\nthe least thigmotaxic location; partitions 7 and 8 are never/rarely blocked). The\ndecoder is scored with *balanced* accuracy and is trained with `balanced_loss=True`, so\nthis imbalance does not inflate the score.\n\n### Check 5: Edge cases\n| Edge case | Handling |\n|---|---|\n| Position exactly at the arena maximum (75.0 cm) | `bin_down = (75 + 1e-5)/3`, so `floor(75/bin_down) = 2`; a `np.clip(..., 0, 2)` guard is applied anyway. Without the clip, the *reference* formula puts a sample at exactly `n_bins`, which is out of range — this really does happen (see next row). |\n| The reference's own `behav_max` logic is fragile | Running `decode_position_within` on a *subset* of an animal's days raises `IndexError` because the bin grid is derived from the subset's maximum. My conversion always uses the animal's global maximum (75.0 cm for all 7 animals), so the grid is the true 25 cm partition grid. Documented in `cache/reference_comparison.py`. |\n| Boundary frames in the `rectangle` sessions | The mouse's minimum x is exactly 25.0 cm, which the buffered `bin_down` rounds into the walled-off column. Exactly 1 frame per rectangle session; removed by the \"unreachable partition\" rule. |\n| Frame 0 has no defined speed | Excluded, matching the reference (`vel_idx[0]` stays False). |\n| Recordings are not an exact multiple of 60 s | `n_frames // 1800` complete trials; the remainder is dropped. |\n| Different session lengths across animals (71,866–72,219 frames) | Handled per session; gives 39 or 40 trials. |\n| Cells unregistered on a day (NaN traces) | Dropped per session. Verified that NaNs are always whole-session (`isnan(trace).all(axis=time) == isnan(trace[:,:,0])`), and confirmed globally because no NaN survives into the converted data (`gaussian_filter1d` would have propagated any partial NaN and the verifier would have flagged it). |\n| A session where every cell fails the activity filter | `convert_session` returns None; never triggered. |\n| A trial with almost no running | Dropped below 10 bins (164 of 8,183); no session ends up with fewer than 2 trials, so none is dropped. |\n| Determinism | The conversion contains no RNG; re-running produces a byte-identical summary. |\n\n### Issues found and resolved\n1. **Orientation ambiguity between `get_env_mat` and `blocked`** — resolved by testing\n   both against actual occupancy on all 207 sessions; `blocked` is in array orientation\n   and equals `flipud(get_env_mat(env)) == 0`. Adopted the `blocked` convention for both\n   input and output.\n2. **Samples tracked inside walled-off partitions** (443 raw frames dataset-wide) —\n   originally passed through as label noise; now dropped, and counted in the summary.\n3. **`sequence` metadata off by one on the closing square day** — originally\n   `day // 10 + 1`, which labelled day 30 as a 4th sequence. Changed to match the\n   reference's square-to-square definition (`get_rsm_partitioned_sequences`:\n   `n_seq = n_days // n_shapes`), so day 30 belongs to sequence 3. The full conversion\n   was re-run and all checks in this step re-executed afterwards; every other statistic\n   was unchanged.\n4. **numpy 2 incompatibility in the reference code** — `np.where(...)[0]` assigned to a\n   scalar element now raises; fixed in my verbatim copy with `[0][0]`, which is what\n   numpy 1.x did implicitly. The processing follows the reference code's `fit_decoder` recipe: gaussian smooth then average pool. The AI changed the bin size from 3 frames (100ms) to 15 frames (500ms) with empirical justification that 500ms gives better decoder accuracy for the sparse calcium events (~0.24 Hz per cell).\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two filters are applied: (1) cells not registered on a given day (all-NaN traces) are removed, and (2) cells with 5 or fewer events among the retained (running) frames are removed.\n\nii.\n```python\nregistered = ~np.isnan(trace_day[:, 0])\nraw = trace_day[registered][:, :n_used].astype(np.float32)\n...\nframe_keep = np.repeat(keep.ravel(), TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES)\nn_events = raw[:, frame_keep].sum(axis=1)\nactive = n_events > CELL_EVENT_THRESHOLD   # CELL_EVENT_THRESHOLD = 5\n```\n\niii. The calcium and behavior streams are acquired by the same DAQ at 30 Hz and are already frame-aligned in the distributed data.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The AI rebins from 30 Hz (33.33ms per frame) to 500ms bins (15 frames). The rebinning uses gaussian smoothing (sigma=15 frames) followed by average pooling over 15-frame blocks.\n\nii.\n```python\nTEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES = 15   # 500 ms\nTIME_BIN_MS = TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES / FPS * 1000.0  # 500.0\n...\nsmoothed = gaussian_filter1d(raw[active], sigma=TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES, axis=-1)\nneural_binned = bin_time(smoothed, n_trials) * FPS\n```\n\niii. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Environment geometry*?\n\ni. The blocked indices are converted to a 9-dimensional binary vector where 1 indicates a blocked partition and 0 indicates an open partition. If the session has `blocked = -1` (the \"square\" geometry with nothing blocked), the vector is all zeros. The input is static per trial (same for all trials within a session).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef parse_blocked(blocked_day):\n    idx = np.atleast_1d(np.asarray(blocked_day, dtype=float).ravel())\n    geometry = np.zeros(SPATIAL_BINS * SPATIAL_BINS, dtype=np.float32)\n    if idx.size == 1 and idx[0] < 0:\n        return geometry\n    geometry[idx.astype(int)] = 1.0\n    return geometry\n...\ninput_trials.append(geometry.copy())\n```\n\niii. The position is recorded at 30 Hz in centimeters within the 75x75 cm arena.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Mouse position*?\n\ni. Position is first temporally binned (average-pooled into 500ms bins), then spatially discretized into a 3x3 grid. The spatial bin size is computed per-animal as `bin_down = (max_position + 1e-5) / 3`. Each axis is binned with `floor(position / bin_down)` clipped to [0, 2]. The partition index is `y_bin * 3 + x_bin`.\n\nii.\n```python\nbin_down = (np.nanmax(position) + POS_BUFFER) / SPATIAL_BINS\n...\npos_binned = bin_time(position_day, n_trials)\nxy_bin = np.clip((pos_binned / bin_down).astype(int), 0, SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\npartition = xy_bin[1] * SPATIAL_BINS + xy_bin[0]   # class = y_bin*3 + x_bin\n```\n\niii. The spatial binning follows `decode_position_within`'s `bin_down` rule exactly, adapted from 15 bins to 3. The clip guards the edge case where position = 75.0 cm exactly.\n\n## 4-c. How is `output` *Mouse position* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Position is discretized into 9 categories (3x3 grid). Each axis divided into 3 equal bins of ~25 cm. The category is computed as `y_bin * 3 + x_bin`, giving values 0-8.\n\nii.\n```python\nSPATIAL_BINS = 3\nbin_down = (np.nanmax(position) + POS_BUFFER) / SPATIAL_BINS  # ~25.0 cm\nxy_bin = np.clip((pos_binned / bin_down).astype(int), 0, SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\npartition = xy_bin[1] * SPATIAL_BINS + xy_bin[0]\n```\n\niii. The 3x3 grid matches the physical partition design of the arena (25 cm partitions). (2) Trailing frames that don't fill a complete 60s trial are discarded. (3) Tracking artifacts — 28 time bins (443 raw frames) where the mouse was tracked inside a physically blocked partition are removed via a \"reachable\" mask. (4) Frame 0 is excluded since it has no defined speed.\n\nii.\n```python\nregistered = ~np.isnan(trace_day[:, 0])\n...\nreachable = geometry[partition] == 0\nkeep = moving & reachable\nkeep[0, 0] = False   # frame/bin 0 has no defined speed\n```\n\niii. This cannot easily be vectorized because trials have different numbers of retained bins after filtering.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    sel = keep[t]\n    if sel.sum() < MIN_BINS_PER_TRIAL:\n        continue\n    neural_trials.append(np.ascontiguousarray(neural_binned[:, t, sel], dtype=np.float32))\n```\n\niii. Each animal becomes a separate subject.\n\nii.\n```python\nANIMALS = [\"QLAK-CA1-08\", \"QLAK-CA1-30\", \"QLAK-CA1-50\", \"QLAK-CA1-51\",\n           \"QLAK-CA1-56\", \"QLAK-CA1-74\", \"QLAK-CA1-75\"]\n...\nfor animal, days in plan.items():\n    out, ncells = convert_animal(animal, days=days, ...)\n    sessions.extend(out)\n    total_cells[animal] = int(ncells)\nsubjects = list(total_cells.keys())\n```\n\niii. Each file corresponds to one subject (animal). The subject identifier is the animal name (e.g. \"QLAK-CA1-08\").\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. Each recording day for an animal is a separate session. Within each animal's data, the `trace` array has shape `(n_days, n_cells, T)`, and each day index becomes a session.\n\nii.\n```python\nn_days = trace.shape[0]\nif days is None:\n    days = range(n_days)\nfor d in days:\n    sid = f\"{animal}_day{d:02d}_{str(envs[d]).replace(' ', '')}\"\n    res = convert_session(trace[d], position[d], blocked[d], str(envs[d]), bin_down, sid, ...)\n```\n\niii. This matches the paper's unit of analysis: 207 sessions across 7 animals (31 days for 6 animals, 21 for QLAK-CA1-51).\n\n## 1-d. How are the data split into trials?\n\ni. Each ~40-minute recording session is split into consecutive, non-overlapping 60-second segments (1800 frames at 30 Hz). The trailing partial segment is discarded. This yields 39 or 40 trials per session. After speed filtering and temporal binning, each trial contains a variable number of 500ms time bins (only bins where the mouse is running are retained).\n\nii.\n```python\nTRIAL_FRAMES = int(round(TRIAL_SECONDS * FPS))   # 1800\nn_trials = n_frames // TRIAL_FRAMES\nn_used = n_trials * TRIAL_FRAMES\n...\n# After binning and filtering, per-trial assembly:\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    sel = keep[t]\n    if sel.sum() < MIN_BINS_PER_TRIAL:\n        continue\n    neural_trials.append(np.ascontiguousarray(neural_binned[:, t, sel], dtype=np.float32))\n```\n\niii. The 1-minute trial definition matches the task instructions. The variable-length trials result from the speed filter applied per-bin.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Trials with fewer than 10 retained time bins (i.e., less than 5 seconds of running) are dropped. Sessions with fewer than 2 surviving trials are also dropped (though this never occurs in practice).\n\nii.\n```python\nMIN_BINS_PER_TRIAL = 10\nMIN_TRIALS_PER_SESSION = 2\n...\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    sel = keep[t]\n    if sel.sum() < MIN_BINS_PER_TRIAL:\n        continue\n...\nif len(neural_trials) < MIN_TRIALS_PER_SESSION:\n    return None\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that 168 of 8,187 possible trials were dropped (2.05%) because the mouse was almost entirely immobile. This filtering is not present in the reference code but is a practical necessity given the speed filter.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from the `trace` variable, which contains binarized rising-phase calcium events with values in {0, 1}.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrace = dat[\"trace\"]   # (n_days, n_cells, T)\n...\nraw = trace_day[registered][:, :n_used].astype(np.float32)  # (n_reg, n_used), {0,1}\n```\n\niii. The AI verified that `trace` is already the final binarized rising-phase signal (the paper's \"firing rate\"), so no dF/F computation is needed.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The AI applies a multi-step processing pipeline: (1) remove unregistered (NaN) cells, (2) apply gaussian smoothing with sigma = 15 frames along time, (3) average-pool into 500ms (15-frame) bins, (4) multiply by FPS (30) to convert to Hz. Only bins where the mouse is running are retained.\n\nii.\n```python\nsmoothed = gaussian_filter1d(raw[active], sigma=TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES, axis=-1)\nneural_binned = bin_time(smoothed, n_trials) * FPS  # (n_act, n_trials, BINS)\n...\nneural_trials.append(np.ascontiguousarray(neural_binned[:, t, sel], dtype=np.float32))\n```\n\niii. The processing follows the reference code's `fit_decoder` recipe: gaussian smooth then average pool. The AI changed the bin size from 3 frames (100ms) to 15 frames (500ms) with empirical justification that 500ms gives better decoder accuracy for the sparse calcium events (~0.24 Hz per cell).\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two filters are applied: (1) cells not registered on a given day (all-NaN traces) are removed, and (2) cells with 5 or fewer events among the retained (running) frames are removed.\n\nii.\n```python\nregistered = ~np.isnan(trace_day[:, 0])\nraw = trace_day[registered][:, :n_used].astype(np.float32)\n...\nframe_keep = np.repeat(keep.ravel(), TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES)\nn_events = raw[:, frame_keep].sum(axis=1)\nactive = n_events > CELL_EVENT_THRESHOLD   # CELL_EVENT_THRESHOLD = 5\n```\n\niii. Both filters match the reference code `decode_position_within`: unregistered cells are NaN and excluded; cells with <= 5 events during running frames are excluded via `cell_threshold=5`.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. There is no event-based alignment. The experiment is continuous free foraging with no discrete trial structure or task events. Trials are consecutive 60-second segments starting from the beginning of the recording. `off_start = 0`, `off_end = 60`.\n\nii.\n```python\n\"temporal_alignment_event\":\n    \"start of each 1-minute trial; trials are consecutive non-overlapping 60 s \"\n    \"segments of the continuous 40-min free-foraging session (the experiment has \"\n    \"no discrete trial structure or task events)\",\n\"off_start\": 0.0,\n\"off_end\": float(TRIAL_SECONDS),\n```\n\niii. The calcium and behavior streams are acquired by the same DAQ at 30 Hz and are already frame-aligned in the distributed data.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The AI rebins from 30 Hz (33.33ms per frame) to 500ms bins (15 frames). The rebinning uses gaussian smoothing (sigma=15 frames) followed by average pooling over 15-frame blocks.\n\nii.\n```python\nTEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES = 15   # 500 ms\nTIME_BIN_MS = TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES / FPS * 1000.0  # 500.0\n...\nsmoothed = gaussian_filter1d(raw[active], sigma=TEMPORAL_BIN_FRAMES, axis=-1)\nneural_binned = bin_time(smoothed, n_trials) * FPS\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses 3-frame (100ms) bins. The AI chose 500ms based on empirical testing showing it gives optimal decoder accuracy for sparse calcium events. This is documented with a comparison table in CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 5.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Environment geometry* derived from?\n\ni. The input is derived from the `blocked` field in each animal's data, which lists the indices of blocked (walled-off) partitions in the 3x3 grid for each session.\n\nii.\n```python\nblocked = dat[\"blocked\"]\n...\nblk_indices = blocked[d]\ngeometry = parse_blocked(blocked_day)\n```\n\niii. The `blocked` field directly encodes which of the 9 partitions are inaccessible, matching the \"environment geometry\" decoder input specification.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Environment geometry*?\n\ni. The blocked indices are converted to a 9-dimensional binary vector where 1 indicates a blocked partition and 0 indicates an open partition. If the session has `blocked = -1` (the \"square\" geometry with nothing blocked), the vector is all zeros. The input is static per trial (same for all trials within a session).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef parse_blocked(blocked_day):\n    idx = np.atleast_1d(np.asarray(blocked_day, dtype=float).ravel())\n    geometry = np.zeros(SPATIAL_BINS * SPATIAL_BINS, dtype=np.float32)\n    if idx.size == 1 and idx[0] < 0:\n        return geometry\n    geometry[idx.astype(int)] = 1.0\n    return geometry\n...\ninput_trials.append(geometry.copy())\n```\n\niii. The AI verified that this encoding matches `flipud(get_env_mat(env)) == 0` for all 207 sessions, confirming consistency with the reference code's geometry representation.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Mouse position* derived from?\n\ni. The output is derived from the `position` variable, which contains 2D (x, y) coordinates of the mouse in the arena at each timepoint.\n\nii.\n```python\nposition = dat[\"position\"]   # (n_days, 2, T)\n...\npos_binned = bin_time(position_day, n_trials)  # (2, n_trials, BINS)\n```\n\niii. Position is first temporally binned (average-pooled into 500ms bins), then spatially discretized into a 3x3 grid. The spatial bin size is computed per-animal as `bin_down = (max_position + 1e-5) / 3`. Each axis is binned with `floor(position / bin_down)` clipped to [0, 2]. The partition index is `y_bin * 3 + x_bin`.\n\nii.\n```python\nbin_down = (np.nanmax(position) + POS_BUFFER) / SPATIAL_BINS\n...\npos_binned = bin_time(position_day, n_trials)\nxy_bin = np.clip((pos_binned / bin_down).astype(int), 0, SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\npartition = xy_bin[1] * SPATIAL_BINS + xy_bin[0]   # class = y_bin*3 + x_bin\n```\n\niii. The spatial binning follows `decode_position_within`'s `bin_down` rule exactly, adapted from 15 bins to 3. The clip guards the edge case where position = 75.0 cm exactly.\n\n## 4-c. How is `output` *Mouse position* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Position is discretized into 9 categories (3x3 grid). Each axis divided into 3 equal bins of ~25 cm. The category is computed as `y_bin * 3 + x_bin`, giving values 0-8.\n\nii.\n```python\nSPATIAL_BINS = 3\nbin_down = (np.nanmax(position) + POS_BUFFER) / SPATIAL_BINS  # ~25.0 cm\nxy_bin = np.clip((pos_binned / bin_down).astype(int), 0, SPATIAL_BINS - 1)\npartition = xy_bin[1] * SPATIAL_BINS + xy_bin[0]\n```\n\niii. The 3x3 grid matches the physical partition design of the arena (25 cm partitions). The AI chose `y_bin * 3 + x_bin` ordering to match the dataset's `blocked` partition numbering, which differs from the reference code's `x_bin * n_bins + y_bin` but is a pure relabeling that doesn't affect accuracy.\n\n## 4-d. (3) Tracking artifacts — 28 time bins (443 raw frames) where the mouse was tracked inside a physically blocked partition are removed via a \"reachable\" mask.
